===== PAGE 3 =====
Preface
These lecture notes were created as a study companion for my final exam in the Calculus
of Variations course, taught by Prof. Guido De Philippis. Honestly, I found the lectures
challenging to follow at times, but his enthusiasm kept my curiosity alive, motivating me
to compile these notes for my own future reference.
Please forgive any inconsistencies throughout the text. I compiled this entire document
under a strict two-week time constraint, and I simply did not have the bandwidth to
polish every single detail.
In the spirit of full transparency: my original handwritten notes were not always perfectly
sharp on the underlying assumptions. Due to the limited preparation time, several parts
of this document were written with the assistance of Gemini 3.1 Pro. For instance, the
statement of the Riemann-Lebesgue Lemma in Lecture 5 was corrected by Gemini, and
much of the raw LATEX code was generated by scanning my handwritten drafts. However,
I deliberately wrote out the proofs myself (with some structural polishing). Letting an
AI generate the proofs would have defeated the entire purpose of this project, which is
for me to truly master the material.
As a disclaimer, I was dealing with some health issues while writing the latter half of
these notes (from Lecture 14 onward). Because I was running out of time to prepare
for my other exams, the L ATEX coding and the structural flow in those later sections
rely much more heavily on Gemini’s assistance as a desperate measure. Even so, I take
full responsibility for any errors in this document, as everything is ultimately based
on my own original handwritten notes. Additionally, I only include the notes from 22
lectures given since the last two lectures about the Plateau problem is optional and I
simply unable to write them down here. However, I attach the handwritten note in my
homepage for the sake of completion.
I would be very happy to discuss this topic further or receive corrections for any errata you
might find. You can reach me via the contact details on my homepage at refrainfr.github.io.
Padova
6 May 2026 Orlando Ferrari
ii

1 Some Calculus of Variations Problems
This lecture serves as a starting point for the topic of Calculus of Variations, we started
from listing classical variational problems which serves as the motivation of the topic itself.
The formulation of the problems itself are mostly derived from physical phenomenon.
Isoperimetric Problems
Among all sets E⊂ Rd with a fixed volume|E| =V1, find the one that minimizes the
perimeter.
Instance of the Problem in R2: Let ∂E =γ(S1), where γ :S1→ R2 is a C1 closed
curve. We define the Area A(γ) and Length L(γ) as:

Plateau Problem (Soap Bubbles)
Given a boundary Γ⊂ Rd, look for a surface Σ such that ∂Σ = Γ and the area of Σ is
minimal.
Problem:
min{Area(Σ) :∂Σ = Γ}
Note that as one changes the meaning of surface area and boundary, the problem changes
as well.

2 The Euler Lagrange Equation
Before starting out, the list of references that is relevant to this course can be consulted
in the Bibliography. Following the contents of this course, the most relevant references
are [Dac07] and [Bra24]. The reader is advised to consult those two books since this note
also follows from those sources, in addition to the lectures given.
Back to Calculus of Variations, we are in particular interested in problems of the form
min
ˆ
Ω
F (x,u (x),Du (x))dx
u∈χ

for F : Ω× Rℓ× (Rℓ⊗ Rd) where
χ ={u : Ω⊆ Rd→ Rℓ satisfying constraints}
Typically there are three types of constraints considered, they are given as follows:
1. Boundary value constraint: u|∂Ω is prescribed.
2. Isoperimetric type constraint:
ˆ
Ω
G(x,u,Du ) is constant for some G.
3. Holonomic constraint: the image of u is presribed. An example of this case is
the prescribed condition for geodesics problem.
Moreover, we may refer F as Lagrangian, integrand, or energy density, we will stay
flexible with convention, but Lagrangian will be emphasized more throughout this notes.
§2.1 Deriving Euler-Lagrange Equations

Notes. The inner product⟨Fp(x, ¯u,D ¯u),Dφ⟩ is the Frobenius inner product, it is the same

of inner product to not be mislead in further technical proofs.
However, there will be instance where I do not emphasize this notion for inner product. The
reader is advised to stay vigilant and try to verify calculations and notions given in this
note.
Before continuing, it is nice to review the result on integration by parts as follows.
Theorem 2.1.1 (Integration by Parts Formula)

Lemma 2.1.2 (Du Bois-Reymond, Fundamental Lemma of Calculus of Variations)
Let f∈L1
loc(Ω) such that

each entry of F is 0 a.e. considering appropriate φ.

We summarize these result as follows.
Theorem 2.1.4 (System of Euler-Lagrange Equations)
Let Ω⊂ Rd be an open domain, and suppose u∈C2(Ω, Rℓ) is a critical point of the
functional
F(u) =
ˆ
Ω
F (x,u (x),Du (x))dx,
where the Lagrangian F∈C2(Ω× Rℓ× Rl×d) is sufficiently smooth. Then u satisfies
the Euler-Lagrange system of equations:
divFp(x,u,Du ) =Fz(x,u,Du ). (EL)
By applying the chain rule to expand the total spatial divergence, this is equivalent
to the following system of second-order partial differential equations (the strong
form):
Fpp :D2u +Fpz·Du + divx(Fp) =Fz, (Strong EL)
where divx(Fp) denotes the divergence ofFp taken strictly with respect to the explicit
spatial variable x.
Remark 2.1.5. The PDE (Strong EL) is elliptic when F is convex in the p variable.

For a hypersurface represented as a graph, the vector field ν = (−Du, 1)p

Mean Curvature (H). Therefore, the equation states that H = 0 everywhere on the
surface.

§3.1 Constant Integral Constraint

Before continuing, given is a review of the implicit function theorem that we will use for
the subsequent theorem.
Theorem 3.1.1 (Implicit Function Theorem)
LetA⊂ Rn×Rm be an open set, and letF :A→ Rm be a continuously differentiable
function of class Ck (k≥ 1). We denote a point in A by (x,y ), where x∈ Rn and
y∈ Rm.
Suppose that (a,b )∈A is a point satisfying:
1. F (a,b ) = 0,
2. The partial Jacobian matrix with respect to y, denoted DyF (a,b )∈ Rm×m, is
invertible (i.e., det(DyF (a,b ))̸= 0).
Then, there exists an open neighborhood U ⊂ Rn of a, an open neighborhood
V ⊂ Rm of b, and a unique function g :U→V such that:
F (x,g (x)) = 0 for all x∈U.
Furthermore, the implicit function g is also of class Ck on U, and its Jacobian
derivative at any x∈U is given by:
Dg(x) =−

DyF (x,g (x))
−1DxF (x,g (x)).

problem for which the absolute minimizer is not a solution to the Euler-Lagrange equation.
§3.4 Modern vs. Classical Calculus of Variations Problems
The modern probabilistic and functional analytic approach to the Calculus of Variations
centers around three core questions:
1. Existence: When do minimizers actually exist?
2. Equations: When do they solve Euler-Lagrange equations, and in which weak or
strong sense?
3. Regularity: Are these minimizers ”better” (more smooth, continuous, integrable)
than a generic function in the admissible space?
21

===== PAGE 28 =====
4 Direct Methods in the Calculus of
Variations
Within this lecture, we will discuss the abstract version of direct methods that is essential
to the modern approach of Calculus of Variations
§4.1 Abstract Direct Methods
We shall start on building our main ingredients: lower semicontinuity and coercivity.
Definition 4.1.1 (Lower Semicontinuity)
Let X be a topological space. A function f :X→ R∪{ +∞} is said to be:
1. lower semicontinuous (l.s.c.) if for any x∈X it holds that
f(x)≤ lim inf
y→x
f(y)
2. sequentially lower semicontinuous if for any x∈ X and any sequence
{xn}⊆ X that converges to x it holds that
f(x)≤ lim inf
n→∞
f(xn)
Remark 4.1.2. Note that the notion of lower semicontinuity is closed under suprema since
for any family of lower semicontinuous functions {fα}, we have
fα(x)≤ lim inf
y→x
fα(y)≤ lim inf
y→x
sup
α
fα(y)
and we can take the supremum over α to conclude sup
α
fα(x)≤ lim inf
y→x
sup
α
fα(y).
Definition 4.1.3 (Coercivity)
Let X be a topological space. A function f :X→ R∪{ +∞} is said to be:
1. coercive if for any t∈ R, the set {f ≤ t} is precompact, i.e. {f≤t} is
compact.
2. sequentially coercive if for any t∈ R, the set{f≤t} is sequentially pre-
compact, i.e. any sequence {xn}⊆{ f≤t} admits a converging subsequence.
Remark 4.1.4.
• It is immediate by definition that l.s.c. implies sequential l.s.c. The converse is
true if X is first countable, i.e. it admits a countable base of neighborhoods for
anyx∈X.
• If X is metrizable, coercivity is equivalent with sequential coercivity.

Definition 4.1.5 (Epigraph)
Let X be a set. The epigraph of a function f :X→ R∪{ +∞} is the set
Epif :={(x,t )∈X× R|f(x)≤t}
which is the set of points in X× R that lies above the graph of f.
Proposition 4.1.6
Let X be a (first countable) topological space. A function f :X→ R∪{ +∞} is
l.s.c if and only if Epi f is closed.
Proof. Let f be l.s.c. and (x,y )∈ Epif, there exists a sequence {(xn,yn)}⊆ Epif such
that xn→x and yn→y whereas f(xn)≤yn for all n∈ N. By lower semicontinuity, by
passing n→∞ yields
f(x)≤ lim inf
n→∞
f(xn)≤ limyn =y.
Hence, (x,y )∈ Epif.
Conversely, let Epif be closed and let x∈X and y <f(x) so that (x,y ) /∈ Epif. Hence,
there exists a neighborhood of ( x,y ), say U× (y−ϵ,y +ϵ) such that all points in such
neighborhood is not contained in Epif. Moreover, the structure of epigraph gives us
(U× (−∞,y +ϵ))∩ Epif = ∅. Therefore, we have f(u)≥ y +ϵ for any u∈ U. Let
{xn}⊆ X converges to x, Forn sufficiently large, xn∈U and therefore,
y +ϵ≤ lim inf
n→∞
f(xn).
We can take ϵ→ 0+ and then y→f(x)− to conclude lower semicontinuity of f. ■
Corollary 4.1.7 (It was put as an exercise)
LetX be a (first countable) topological space and f :X→ R∪{+∞}. The following
statements are equivalent:
1. f is l.s.c.
2. {f≤t} is closed for any t∈ R
3. {f >t} is open for any t∈ R
4. Epi f is closed in X× R
5. (X× R)\ Epif ={(x,t )∈X× R :f(x)>t} is open in X× R
Proof. Omitted. The most tedious equivalence (1. ⇔ 4.) has been proved in Proposition
4.1.6. ■

Exercise 4.1.9
Show that a function f : Rd→ [0, +∞] is coercive if and only if lim
|x|→∞
f(x) =∞.
Proof. (⇒) Suppose that f is coercive, for any M∈ R, the set{f≤M} is precompact.
Therefore, there exists R > 0 such that {f ≤ M} ⊆ {|x| ≤R}. Hence, for any
{|x|>R}⊆{ f >M} and we just proved lim
|x|→∞
f(x) =∞.
(⇐) Suppose that lim
|x|→∞
f(x) =∞. Therefore, for any M∈ R, there exists R> 0 such
that
{|x|>R}⊆{ f >M} ⇐⇒ {f≤M}⊆{| x|≤ R}.
Hence,{f≤M} is precompact. ■
Remark 4.1.10. There might be an issue with consistency on the notion of coercivity
from my lecture. The above result also holds for any Banach space E if we define
coercive as having{f≤t} bounded for any t∈ R. Hence, in the case of Banach space,
we use this notion of coercivity instead of the one we defined.
With our ingredients set, we can naturally prove the following result which we also refer
to as the (abstract) direct method.
Theorem 4.1.11 (Weierstrass Method)
Let X be a topological space, f :X→ R∪{ +∞} such that
1. f is (sequentially) lowersemicontinuous
2. f is (sequentially) coercive, i.e., {f ≤ t} is (sequentially) compact for any
t∈ R.
Then either f≡ +∞ or there exists x0∈X such that f(x0) = inf{f(x) :x∈X}<
+∞.
Proof. Suppose there exists x0∈X such that f(x0)<∞. We set m = inf
x∈X
f(x)<∞.
We a minimizing sequence{xn} such that f(xn)→m. Without loss of generality, we
assume{xn}⊆{ f≤m + 1}. By coercivity, xn→ ¯x up to a subsequence for some ¯x∈X.
Therefore, by lower semicontinuity we conclude
m≤f(¯x)≤ lim inf
n→∞
f(xn) =m.

f(x)>−∞. ■
Remark 4.1.12. Givenf :X→ R∪{ +∞}, finding a topology for X for which f is both
coercive and l.s.c. might not be possible and these are conflicting requirements. Indeed,
l.s.c. is easy when we have lots of open sets and compactness is easy with few open sets.
§4.2 Recap on Weak/Weak ∗ Topologies on Banach Space
In this section, we consider E to be a Banach space and E∗ is the (topological) dual of
E. We denote the duality pairing
⟨x∗,x⟩ :=x∗(x) ∀x∗∈E∗,∀x∈E.
Definition 4.2.1
Let E be a Banach space.
1. We say that a sequence {xn}⊂ E weakly converges to x∈ E, denoted by
xn⇀x , if
⟨x∗,xn⟩→⟨ x∗,x⟩ ∀ x∗∈E∗.
2. We say that a sequence {x∗

x∗

⟨x∗
n,x⟩→⟨ x∗,x⟩ ∀ x∈E.
Proposition 4.2.2 (Banach-Steinhaus (Uniform Boundedness Theorem))
Let E be a Banach space.
1. If xn⇀x , then sup

Theorem 4.2.3
Let E be a Banach space and let f :E→ R be a convex and l.s.c. (with respect
to the norm/strong topology) function. Then, f is l.s.c. with respect to the weak
topology.
Proof. To show that f is weakly l.s.c., it is necessary and sufficient to prove that for any
arbitrary real number t∈ R, the sublevel set
Kt ={x∈E|f(x)≤t}
is closed with respect to the weak topology.
From our hypotheses, we establish two critical properties of Kt:
1. Kt is convex: Let x,y∈Kt and λ∈ [0, 1]. By the convexity of f, we have:
f(λx + (1−λ)y)≤λf(x) + (1−λ)f(y)≤λt + (1−λ)t =t
Thus,λx + (1−λ)y∈Kt, proving the set is convex.
2. Kt is norm-closed: By Corollary 4.1.7, in the norm (strong) topology, Kt is
strongly closed.
A fundamental corollary of the Hahn-Banach Separation Theorem (often linked to Mazur’s
Lemma) dictates that for any convex subset of a locally convex topological vector space
(such as a Banach space), closure in the strong topology is equivalent to closure
in the weak topology . (one can consult most functional analysis text such as [ Bre11]
regarding this theorem or follow the lecture of functional analysis in the department!)
Because the set Kt is both convex and strongly closed, it is automatically weakly closed
and thus f is weakly l.s.c. ■
Theorem 4.2.4 (Banach-Alaoglu)
LetE be a Banach space, then the set {∥x∥E′≤R} is weak∗ compact for any R≥ 0.
Proof. Omitted. One may consider most functional analysis textbook such as [ Bre11] for
the proof. ■
Corollary 4.2.5
Let E be a Banach space and f :E∗→ R∪{ +∞}. Then tbe following statements
are equivalent.
1. f is weak∗ coercive
2. lim
∥x∗∥E′→∞
f(x∗) = +∞
3. There exists a monotone function φ : [0, +∞]→ [0, +∞] where φ↑∞ such
that f(x)≥φ(∥x∗∥E′).

Proof. The proof of 3. =⇒ 2. =⇒ 1. follows by the aid of Exercise 4.1.9 trivially.
1. =⇒ 2. Assume otherwise, there exists a sequence {x∗
n}⊆ such that∥x∗

monotonely increasing and converges to +∞ as s→ +∞. ■
From all of our consideration, we can assert the direct methods for reflexive Banach space
as follows.
Theorem 4.2.6 (Direct Methods, E reflexive Banach space)
Let E be a reflexive Banach space, i.e. E∼=E∗∗ and f :E→ R∪{ +∞}, f̸≡ +∞
such that:
1. f is convex
2. f is strongly l.s.c.
3. f is coercive, i.e., lim
∥x∥E→∞
f(x) = +∞
Then, f admits a minimum.
Proof. We shall apply the abstract direct method (Theorem 4.1.11) to the space X =E
endowed with the weak topology. To do so, we must verify that f is both weakly lower
semicontinuous and weakly coercive.
It is immediate from hypotheses 1. and 2., we have f weakly l.s.c. by Theorem 4.2.3.
Hence, we only need to prove weak coercivity.
We must show that for any t∈ R, the sublevel set Kt ={f≤t} is weakly compact. By
hypothesis 3., As established in Exercise 4.1.9, this is equivalent to say every sublevel
set Kt is bounded in the norm topology, meaning there exists some R >0 such that
Kt⊆{∥x∥E≤R}. Since E is a reflexive Banach space (E∼=E∗∗), the weak topology on
E coincides exactly with the weak∗ topology on E∗∗. By the Banach-Alaoglu Theorem,
the norm-closed ball {∥x∥E≤ R} is weakly compact. Furthermore, from Step 1, f is
weakly l.s.c., which guarantees that its sublevel set Kt is weakly closed (by Corollary
4.1.7). Because Kt is a weakly closed subset of a weakly compact set (the closed bounded
ball), Kt itself must be weakly compact. This confirms that f is weakly coercive. ■
Remark 4.2.7. Note that the theorem still holds even when we apply to a function
f :C→ R∪{ +∞} where C⊆E is a convex set by considering ˜f :E→ R∪{ +∞} as
˜f(x) :=
(
f(x) , x∈C
+∞ , x /∈C.
The function ˜f still maintains the same property we needed (convex, strongly l.s.c.,
strongly coercive).
Remark 4.2.8. Hypotheses 1. and 2. of Theorem 4.2.6 play well with weak topology by
Theorem 4.2.3 and hypothesis 3. plays well with weak ∗ topology by Corollary 4.2.5. This
is the reason we require E to be reflexive to make the two topologies coincide.

Theorem 5.2.6
Let Ω⊂ Rd be a bounded measurable set, and let p∈ (1,∞). Suppose f : Rℓ→
R∪{ +∞} is a Borel measurable function that is bounded from below.
Define the integral functionalF :Lp(Ω; Rℓ)→ R∪{ +∞} by
F(u) =
ˆ
Ω
f(u(x))dx.
Then, the following statements are equivalent:
1. F is l.s.c. with respect to the weak topology on Lp(Ω; Rℓ).
2. f is convex and lower semi-continuous on Rℓ.
Proof. (2. =⇒ 1.): Fortunately,Lp is a Banach space. We can utilize Theorem 4.2.3 to
immediately prove this direction. Since f is convex,F is trivially convex. Furthermore,
because f is l.s.c. and bounded from below (and Ω is bounded), we can apply Fatou’s
Lemma to deduce that the map
Lp∋u7→
ˆ
Ω
f(u(x))dx
is l.s.c. in the strong topology of Lp. SinceF is convex and strongly l.s.c., it is l.s.c. with
respect to the weak topology.

Remark 5.2.7 (The Bolza Example and Convexity) . The Bolza problem perfectly il-
lustrates why convexity with respect to the derivative (momentum) variable is strictly
required for weak lower semicontinuity. Consider the minimization problem:
inf
ˆ 1
0
 
((u′)2− 1)2 +u2
dx :u(0) = 0,u (1) = 0

The LagrangianF (x,u,u′) = ((u′)2−1)2 +u2 is not convex with respect tou′ because of
the “double-well potential” ((u′)2− 1)2, which strongly prefers slopes of exactly +1 or−1.
Simultaneously, theu2 term prefers the function itself to be exactly 0. To minimize both
terms, an infimizing sequence {uk} must oscillate wildly between slopes of +1 and −1
(creating a microscopic zigzag/sawtooth pattern) to keep the derivative energy near zero,
while compressing the amplitude of these zigzags so that the function stays arbitrarily
close to the x-axis (uk≈ 0).
As k→∞ , this sequence converges uniformly (and thus weakly in W 1,2) to the flat line:
¯u(x)≡ 0

potential.

However, if we evaluate the energy of this limit function:
F(¯u) =

in W 1,2 strictly because the double-well potential breaks convexity.

6 Recap on Sobolev Space
The needed proofs for the mentioned results in Section 6.1 can be consulted from [ Eva10]
and [Bre11]. However, those two references only considered the scalar-valued result.
Nonetheless, the generalization to vector-valued functions follows canonically.
§6.1 Preliminary Properties of Sobolev Space
Let Ω⊆ Rd be an open set (often assumed to have a Lipschitz boundary for embedding
and trace theorems, but not required for basic definitions).
Definition 6.1.1 (Sobolev Space W 1,p)
For 1≤p≤∞ , the Sobolev space W 1,p(Ω; Rℓ) is defined as:


u∈Lp(Ω; Rℓ) :
∃g∈Lp(Ω; Rℓ×d) such thatˆ
Ω
ui(x)∂jφ(x)dx =−
ˆ
Ω
gij(x)φ(x)dx ∀φ∈C∞
c (Ω; R)


.
The function g is unique (up to a set of measure zero), and we denote the weak
derivative asDu =g.
We define the Sobolev norm to the Sobolev space W 1,p as
∥u∥W 1,p(Ω;Rℓ) =∥u∥p +∥Du∥p≃
ˆ
Ω
|u|pdx +
ˆ
Ω
|Du|pdx
1/p
.
Remark 6.1.2.
1. (W 1,p(Ω; Rℓ),∥·∥W 1,p(Ω;Rℓ)) is a Banach space.
2. W 1,2(Ω; Rℓ) is a Hilbert space with
⟨u,w⟩W 1,2(Ω;Rℓ) =
ˆ
Ω
⟨u,v⟩ +⟨Du,Dv⟩ dx
where⟨Du,Dv⟩ is the Frobenius inner product.
3. Alternatively, one can define the space H1,p(Ω; Rℓ) as the completion of smooth
functions C∞(Ω; Rℓ) with respect to the Sobolev norm ∥·∥W 1,p(Ω;Rℓ)) and obviously
H1,p(Ω; Rℓ)⊆W 1,p(Ω; Rℓ)
Theorem 6.1.3 (Meyers-Serrin, 1964)
Let Ω⊆ Rd be any open set . (Crucially, no regularity on the boundary ∂Ω is
required). Then smooth functions are dense in W 1,p(Ω), meaning:
H1,p(Ω; Rℓ) =W 1,p(Ω; Rℓ) for 1 ≤p< ∞.

Remark 6.1.4. For Meyers-Serrin Theorem, it is important we define H1,p(Ω; Rℓ) on
completion over C∞(Ω; Rℓ) and not other spaces. For instance, approximation by
C1(Ω; Rℓ) generally fails if Ω has an irregular or disconnected boundary.
Example (The Split Disc): Let D⊂ R2 be the open unit disc and L ={(x, 0) :−1<
x< 1} be its horizontal diameter. Define Ω = D\L. The function

sequence vk∈C∞(¯Ω), because vk must be uniformly continuous across the slit L, which
would force the gradient norm ∥Dvk∥Lp→∞ to accommodate the jump discontinuity.

Theorem 6.1.5 (Properties on Lipschitz Domains)
If Ω⊂ Rd is a bounded open set with a Lipschitz boundary, then:
1. Global Extension: There exists a continuous, linear extension operator
Ext :W 1,p(Ω; Rℓ)→W 1,p(Rd; Rℓ) such that Ext(u)|Ω =u a.e.
2. Density: C1(¯Ω; Rℓ) is dense in W 1,p(Ω; Rℓ).
3. Trace Operator: There exists a unique continuous linear operator tr :
W 1,p(Ω; Rℓ)→Lp(∂Ω; Rℓ) extending the classical restriction tr(u) =u|∂Ω for
u∈C0(¯Ω)∩W 1,p(Ω).
4. Gauss-Green Formula: For allu∈W 1,p(Ω; Rℓ) and φ∈C1(¯Ω):
ˆ
Ω

Theorem 6.1.9 (Rellich-Kondrachov)
The embedding (6.1) is compact. That is, if {uk} is a bounded sequence in
W 1,p(Rd; Rℓ), then there exists a subsequence {uk′} and a function u∈W 1,p(Rd; Rℓ)
such that for any ball BR⊆ Rd:
∥uk′−u∥Lq(BR;Rℓ)→ 0 as k′→∞
Furthermore, ifp≥d, the same is true for q =∞ (implying uniform convergence on
compact sets).

Remark 6.1.13 (Weak Convergence). For 1<p< ∞, the Sobolev space W 1,p(Ω; Rℓ) (and
its zero-trace counterpart W 1,p
0 (Ω; Rℓ)) is a reflexive Banach space. The weak topology is
characterized as follows:

Remark 6.1.14 (Compactness). By the Rellich-Kondrachov compactness theorem (and
the uniform boundedness principle), if uk ⇀u weakly in W 1,p, then uk→u strongly in
Lq
loc. If Ω is bounded and Lipschitz, this strong convergence holds globally in Lq(Ω) for
1≤q <p∗.

§6.2 Application of the Sobolev Space to the Abstract Direct
Methods
Theorem 6.2.1 (Existence of Minimizers)
Let Ω⊂ Rd be a bounded open set with Lipschitz boundary. Let f : Ω×Rℓ×Rℓ×d→
R be a Lagrangian f(x,z,ξ ) satisfying:
1. x7→f(x,z,ξ ) is Lebesgue measurable.
2. (z,ξ )7→f(x,z,ξ ) is continuous and convex for a.e. x∈ Ω.
3. Coercivity:|f(x,z,ξ )|≥ C|ξ|p−M|z|q−h(x) for some 1≤q <p,C,M >0,
and h∈L1(Ω).

Proposition 6.2.4 (Euler-Lagrange Equations and Further Properties)
Suppose the hypotheses of the previous theorem hold, and let ¯u∈g +W 1,p
0 (Ω; Rℓ)
be a minimizer of F. Depending on further growth conditions on f, the following
properties hold:
(a) Absence of Lavrentiev Phenomenon: If we additionally assume the upper
bound|f(x,z,ξ )|≤ α(x) +C (|ξ|p +|z|p) where α∈L1(Ω), then the infimum
over smooth functions matches the Sobolev minimum:
inf
u∈C1(¯Ω;Rℓ)
u=g on ∂Ω
F(u) = min
u∈W 1,p(Ω;Rℓ)
u=g on ∂Ω
F(u)
(b) Euler-Lagrange (E-L) Equations: If

 

where β∈Lp′

0 (Ω; Rℓ)
(c) Sufficiency: By convexity of f, any solution to the weak (E-L) equation is a
minimizer.
(d) Uniqueness: If the function f is either strictly convex in z or in ξ, then the
minimizer is unique.
Remark 6.2.5. This condition posed in (a) and (b) is a generalization of the conditions
mentioned in the lectures. This coercivity condition follows from Theorem 3.30 of [ Dac07].
Remark 6.2.6 (Context and Pathologies) .
(i) Assumption (a) ensures that relaxing the problem from C1 toW 1,p does not strictly
decrease the infimum. When this fails, it is called the Lavrentiev Phenomenon.

(iii) Statement (b) simply provides the growth conditions under which we can rigorously
differentiate the functional to derive the E-L equations.

7 Existence of Solution to Variational
Problem: Proof of Theorem 6.2.1 and
Proposition 6.2.4
§7.1 Proof of Existence
The structure of the proof is divided into three steps: proving F is well-defined;F is
coercive;F is lower semisemicontinuous. With these three steps done, we can invoke
direct methods (Theorem 4.1.11) to prove Theorem 6.2.1
ProvingF is well-defined. It is sufficient to prove x7→f(x,u (x),Du (x)) is a measurable
map.
Notice that the map x 7→ f(x,u (x),Du (x)) is the composition of two maps: x 7→
(u(x),Du (x)) which is measurable immediately and (z(x),ξ (x))7→f(x,u (x),ξ (x)) where
z(x),ξ (x) measurable.
To prove measurability of (z(x),ξ (x))7→f(x,u (x),ξ (x)), we start from the case (z(x),ξ (x))
is a simple function, i.e.
(z(x),ξ (x)) =
nX
j=1
(zj,ξj)1Ej.
We have
f(x,z (x),ξ (x)) =
nX
j=1
f(x,zj,ξj)1Ej.
From assumption 1., x7→f(x,z,ξ ) is measurable. Therefore, f(x,z (x),ξ (x)) is measur-
able.
To prove the general case, let ( z(x),ξ (x)) be measurable. There exists a sequence of
simple functions sn(x) converging pointwise to (z(x),ξ (x)) almost everywhere. Because
(z,ξ )7→f(x,z,ξ ) is continuous for a.e.x, we havef(x,sn(x))→f(x,z (x),ξ (x)) pointwise
almost everywhere. Since pointwise limits of measurable functions are measurable, the
composition is measurable. ■
Remark 7.1.1. ProvingF is well-defined is an important matter, there is a casef(x,z (x),ξ (x))
is not measurable. Let E⊆ [0, 1] be a non-measurable set. We consider ˜E ={(x,y )∈
E×E :x =y}⊆ [0, 1]2. Hence, the function f(x,z,ξ ) = 1− 1 ˜E(x,z ) is not measurable
even though it is still lower semicontinuous and thus F is not well-defined.

§7.1.2 Proving Lower Semicontinuity of F
It is sufficient to proveF is convex and strongly l.s.c., i.e. l.s.c. with respect to the norm
topology of W 1,p(Ω; Rℓ) and thus we have lower semicontinuity in the weak topology
from Theorem 4.2.3.
Indeed, by assumption 2., since (z,ξ )7→f(x,z,ξ ) is convex for a.e. x∈ Ω, the convexity
ofF is trivial.

§7.2.1 Continuity of F
Proof of Theorem 6.2.1 (a). We assume the upper growth condition|f(x,z,ξ )|≤ C(|ξ|p+
|z|p) +α(x) holds with α∈L1(Ω). With this assumption, we can prove F is continuous

pointwise almost everywhere. By the continuity of f in its second and third arguments,
we have:
f(x,uk(x),Duk(x))→f(x,u (x),Du (x)) a.e. x∈ Ω
By the upper growth condition, the sequence is bounded pointwise by:
|f(x,uk,Duk)|≤ C(|uk(x)|p +|Duk(x)|p) +α(x) :=Hk(x)
Because uk→u strongly in W 1,p, the dominating sequence Hk(x) converges strongly in
L1(Ω) to the limit function H(x) :=C(|u(x)|p +|Du(x)|p) +α(x).
Since fk→ f a.e. and is dominated by an L1-convergent sequence Hk→ H, we can

§7.2.3 Sufficiency of the Euler-Lagrange Equations
Proof of Theorem 6.2.1 (c). We wish to show that if f is convex in (z,ξ ) and ¯u satisfies
the Euler-Lagrange equations, then ¯u is a minimizer.
Recall the first-order condition for convexity: if a function F :V → R on a vector space
is differentiable and convex, it satisfies F (a)≥F (b) +⟨∇F (b),a−b⟩ for all a,b∈V .
Applying this pointwise to the integrand f(x,·,·) for almost every x∈ Ω, and taking any
admissible competitor v∈g +W 1,p
0 (Ω; Rℓ), we have:

+⟨Dzf(x, ¯u(x),D ¯u(x)), (v(x)− ¯u(x))⟩
+⟨Dξf(x, ¯u(x),D ¯u(x)), (Dv(x)−D¯u(x))⟩

§7.2.4 Uniqueness Under Strict Convexity
Proof of Theorem 6.2.1 (d). Assume for contradiction that there exist two distinct mini-
mizers u̸=v in g +W 1,p
0 (Ω; Rℓ). Let m =F(u) =F(v) = minF.
Since the admissible space is affine, the arithmetic mean w = 1
2(u +v) is also admissible.
By the convexity ofF:
m≤F (w)≤ 1
2F(u) + 1
2F(v) =m
This forcesF(w) =m. Rewriting this as a single integral yields:
ˆ
Ω
1
2f(x,u,Du ) + 1
2f(x,v,Dv )−f(x,w,Dw )

dx = 0
Because f is convex, the integrand is non-negative, so it must be zero almost everywhere:
f(x,w,Dw ) = 1
2f(x,u,Du ) + 1
2f(x,v,Dv ) a.e. in Ω
We now obtain a contradiction depending on the strict convexity assumption:
• If f is strictly convex in z: Since u̸= v in Lp, there exists a set E⊂ Ω of
positive measure where u(x)̸=v(x). On E, strict convexity enforcesf(x,w,Dw )<
1
2f(x,u,Du ) + 1
2f(x,v,Dv ), contradicting the a.e. equality.
• If f is strictly convex in ξ: Since (u−v)∈W 1,p
0 (Ω; Rℓ) and u̸=v, Poincar´ e’s
inequality ensures∥Du−Dv∥Lp > 0. Thus, Du̸=Dv on a set of positive measure,
again triggering a strict pointwise inequality that contradicts the a.e. equality.
Therefore, u =v almost everywhere, and the minimizer is unique. ■

10. Pathologies and Limitations of the Classical Assumptions
The standard assumptions (p> 1 and convexity in ξ) are sometimes too restrictive for
physical and geometric realities:
• Minimal Surfaces: The functional
ˆ
Ω
p
1 +|Du|2dx has linear growth (p = 1).
Bounded energy sequences belong to W 1,1, which is not reflexive. Weak limits may
exhibit jump discontinuities, requiring the problem to be relaxed into the space of
Bounded Variation (BV ).
• Lack of Convexity in z: Phase transition models often utilize double-well
potentials, such as the Allen-Cahn energy
ˆ
Ω
 

• Lack of Convexity in ξ: In nonlinear elasticity (e.g., Neo-Hookean materi-
als), the energy depends on the deformation volume, yielding functionals likeˆ
Ω

ing weaker topological conditions such as polyconvexity to establish lower semi-
continuity.

9 Beyond Convexity - 1
§9.1 Is Convexity Necessary in the Gradient Variable?
Recall that the Direct Method (Theorem 4.1.11) relies on two pillars:
1. Coercivity (Growth conditions and lower bounds) to guarantee a bounded mini-
mizing sequence.
2. Weak Lower Semicontinuity (L.S.C.) to ensure the limit of the sequence
minimizes the functional.
Previously in, we established that if f(x,z,ξ ) is jointly convex in ( z,ξ ), thenF(u) =ˆ
Ω
f(x,u,Du )dx is convex. By abstract nonsense (Theorem 4.2.3), strong l.s.c. combined
with convexity implies weak l.s.c. for F
However, requiring convexity in the state variable z is extremely restrictive (it excludes
phase transition models, double-well potentials, etc. as shown in the previous lecture).
We need to relax this.
Remark 9.1.1 (The Role of Oscillations) . If uk ⇀ u weakly in W 1,p(Ω), the Rellich-
Kondrachov compactness theorem ensures that uk→u strongly in Lp(Ω) (for bounded
Ω). Consequently, up to a subsequence, uk→u pointwise almost everywhere.
Because the sequence{uk} converges pointwise, it does not suffer from high-frequency
oscillations. The weak convergence Duk ⇀Du , however, means the gradient can oscillate
heavily. Therefore, we strictly need convexity in the gradient variable ξ to “absorb” these
oscillations, but we do not need convexity in the state variable z.
Theorem 9.1.2 (Morrey-Tonelli)
Let Ω ⊂ Rd be a domain of finite measure. Let f : Ω× Rℓ× Rℓ×d → R be a
Lagrangian satisfying the Carath´ eodory conditions:
1. x7→f(x,z,ξ ) is Lebesgue measurable for all ( z,ξ ).
2. (z,ξ )7→f(x,z,ξ ) is continuous for almost every x∈ Ω.
Assume further that:
(3) Lower Bound: f is non-negative (or bounded below by an L1(Ω) function).
(4) Partial Convexity: For almost every x ∈ Ω and all z ∈ Rℓ, the map
ξ7→f(x,z,ξ ) is convex.
Then, the integral functional F(u) =
ˆ
Ω
f(x,u (x),Du (x))dx is well-defined and
weakly lower semicontinuous with respect to the weak topology of W 1,p(Ω) for any
p≥ 1.

Proposition 9.2.5
Let Ω⊂ Rd be an open set with C1 boundary and let ψ1, ψ2∈C1(Ω; Rℓ) such that
ψ1 and ψ2 agrees in ∂Ω. Then, for any m×m minorMm where 1≤m≤τ(ℓ,d ),
we have ˆ
Ω
Mm(Dψ1)dx =
ˆ
Ω
Mm(Dψ2)dx
Proof. Without loss of generality, it suffices to consider the principal case where the
dimensions of the domain and codomain are equal to m (i.e., Ω⊂ Rm and ℓ =d =m),
and the minor Mm(A) is the determinant det(A). The general theorem follows by
applying this exact argument to arbitrary m×m submatrices of the full Jacobian.
The key to establishing the equality of these integrals is recognizing that the determinant
of a Jacobian is a null Lagrangian. In the language of differential geometry, it can be
expressed as an exact form.
1. The Determinant as a Pullback
Let ω0 = dy1∧···∧ dym be the standard volume form on the target space Rm. The
pullback of ω0 by a sufficiently smooth function ψ is given by:
ψ∗ω0 =dψ1∧···∧ dψm
By expanding each differential asdψi =
mX
k=1
∂ψi
∂xkdxk and applying the alternating property
of the wedge product ( dxi∧dxj =−dxj∧dxi), this pullback naturally isolates the
determinant of the Jacobian matrix:
ψ∗ω0 = det(Dψ)dx1∧···∧ dxm
2. Exactness and the Piola Identity
Assume temporarily that ψ∈C2(Ω; Rm). The volume form ω0 is exact on Rm. Because
the exterior derivative commutes with pullbacks, ψ∗ω0 must also be exact. We can
explicitly construct the (m− 1)-form αψ such that dαψ =ψ∗ω0:
det(Dψ)dx1∧···∧ dxm =dψ1∧···∧ dψm
=d
 

=d
 
(−1)j+1ψjdψ1∧···∧ dψj−1∧dψj+1∧···∧ dψm
By averaging these symmetrized forms over all m indices, we obtain the differential
representation of the Piola identity:
det(Dψ)dx1∧···∧ dxm =d

 1

(In classical vector calculus, the exactness d2 = 0 corresponds to the row-wise divergence-
free property of the cofactor matrix,div(cof(Dψ)) = 0, leading to det(Dψ) = div(cof(Dψ)ψ)).
3. Applying Stokes’ Theorem for Boundary Agreement

4. Extension to C1 via Density
The proposition assumes ψ1,ψ 2∈C1, but the construction of the exact form and the
nilpotency (d2 = 0) require second weak derivatives. However, C2(Ω) is dense in C1(Ω).
We can approximateψ1 andψ2 by sequences of C2 functions that maintain the boundary
agreement. Because the integral of the determinant is continuous with respect to the C1
topology, the equality extends toC1(Ω; Rm) via a standard density argument, completing
the proof. ■

§10.1 Observations on Rank-One Convexity and Quasiconvexity
Recalling the definition of rank-one convexity, we can also write rank-one convexity as
the condition of the mapping
t7→g(A +t(p⊗q))
is convex for any A∈ Rℓ⊗ Rd, p∈ Rℓ, and q∈ Rd.
Remark 10.1.1. We summarized our current result (and goal) as follows
f convex = ⇒ f polyconvex = ⇒ f quasiconvex = ⇒ f rank-one convex
⇍ ⇍ ⇍ (for l≥ 3 by ˇSver´ ak)
Following Remark 9.2.2, if l = 1, we have all notions being equivalent. However, the case
l = 2 it is still an open problem (Morrey conjecture).
We only need to proof quasiconvexity implies rank-one convexity to settle our chain of
implications.

Our objective is to demonstrate the rank-one convexity inequality: f(A)≤ λf(A1) +

of A and the rank-one direction a⊗b:
A1 =A + (1−λ)a⊗b,
A2 =A−λa⊗b.
To utilize the quasiconvexity of f, we construct a highly oscillating sequence of test
functions (a laminate microstructure). We begin by defining a 1-periodic, piecewise affine

and that h is continuous and globally bounded.

11 Beyond Convexity - 3
In the modern calculus of variations, particularly in the study of microstructure and
materials science, we often encounter the problem of differential inclusions. Instead of
solving a standard partial differential equation, we seek a function whose gradient takes
values in a prescribed set of matrices.
Remark 11.0.1 (Exact and Approximate Inclusions) . This leads to two foundational
problems:
• Exact Inclusions: Given a set of “allowed states” K⊆ Rl⊗ Rd, we seek to under-
stand the structure of functions Ψ ∈W 1,∞(Ω; Rℓ) satisfying boundary conditions
such that the weak derivative satisfies DΨ(x)∈K for almost every x∈ Ω. Equiva-
lently, one can look for a matrix field V ∈L∞(Ω; Rℓ⊗ Rd) such that V (x)∈K a.e.
and curlV = 0 in the sense of distributions.
• Approximate Inclusions: Given the same set K, we study minimizing sequences
or sequences of functions{Ψk} such that the L∞-distance to the set K vanishes,
i.e., dist(DΨk,K )→ 0 in measure or almost everywhere.
The simplest, yet highly non-trivial, instance of these problems occurs when the target
set K consists of exactly two matrices, K ={A,B}.
Let Ω⊂ Rd be an open, bounded, and connected domain. Let Lip(Ω; Rl) denote the space
of Lipschitz continuous functions, which is isomorphic to the Sobolev space W 1,∞(Ω; Rl).
Theorem 11.0.2 (Ball-James)
Let Ψ∈ Lip(Ω; Rl) be such that its weak derivative satisfies DΨ(x)∈{ A,B} for
almost every x∈ Ω, where A,B∈ Rl×d.
a) If rank(A− B) ≥ 2, then the gradient must be strictly constant almost
everywhere: either DΨ≡A a.e. or DΨ≡B a.e.
b) If{Ψk}∞
k=1 is a uniformly bounded sequence of functions (i.e., sup
k
∥DΨk∥L∞ <
∞) and Lipschitz such that dist(DΨk,{A,B})→ 0 almost everywhere, then,
up to a subsequence, either
ˆ
Ω
|DΨk−A|dx→ 0 or
ˆ
Ω
|DΨk−B|dx→ 0.
Proof of part a) in 2D. To illustrate the rigidity imposed by the rank-two condition, we
provide the proof for Part (a) in the specific case where l = d = 2. Let Ω ⊂ R2 be a
connected domain. We assume without loss of generality that DΨ(x)∈{A,B} for almost
everyx∈ Ω.
Define the set E = {x ∈ Ω : DΨ(x) = B}. The complement up to a null set is
Ω\E ={x∈ Ω :DΨ(x) =A}. We can express the gradient globally using the indicator
function 1E of the set E:
DΨ =A + (B−A)1E. (11.1)

A fundamental theorem in the calculus of distributions states that a function whose
distributional gradient vanishes on a connected domain must be a constant almost
everywhere. Consequently, 1E must be constant. Since it is an indicator function, its
only possible values are 0 or 1.

a.e.), establishing the rigid phase separation. ■

as follows.

with Lipschitz boundary. Then, the functional F(u) =

f(Du)dx is sequentially

Proposition 11.2.2
Let p>ℓ ∧d and suppose uk ⇀u weakly in W 1,p(Ω; Rl). Then for the mapping of
minorsM, we have
M(Duk)⇀M(Du) weakly in Lp/d(Ω).
Remark 11.2.3. Actually, a more general statement is true: If uk ⇀u weakly in W 1,p
and m≤ min{p,ℓ,d}, then the M-th order minors converge weakly, i.e.,
Mm(Duk)⇀Mm(Du) weakly in Lp/m(Ω)
even when p/m = 1. However, for the critical case p/m = 1, one need different approach
from the proof presented here.
Proof of Ball’s Theorem (Assuming Proposition 11.2.2 is True). Since f is polyconvex,
there exists a convex function g such that f(ξ) =g(M(ξ)) for all matrices ξ. To ensure
the integrals are well-defined and Fatou’s lemma can be applied, we assume g is bounded
from below.
Let uk ⇀u weakly in W 1,p(Ω; Rl) for p>d . By the assumed Proposition, the sequence
of minors converges weakly in the Banach space Lp/d(Ω):
M(Duk)⇀M(Du) weakly in Lp/d(Ω).
Now, we define the functional G(v) =
ˆ
Ω
g(v(x))dx for v∈ Lp/d(Ω). We analyze the
properties ofG to justify the weak lower semicontinuity:
1. Convexity: Since the integrand g is a convex function, the functional G is strictly
convex on Lp/d(Ω).
2. Strong Lower Semicontinuity: Because g is convex on a finite-dimensional
space, it is continuous. If a sequence vk→ v strongly in Lp/d(Ω), there exists a
subsequence vkj that converges to v pointwise almost everywhere and it attains the
limit inferior of{G(vk)}. Applying Fatou’s Lemma (using the lower bound of g),
we get:
lim inf
k→∞
G(vk) = lim inf
j→∞
ˆ
Ω
g(vkj(x))dx≥
ˆ
Ω
g(v(x))dx =G(v).
Since this holds for any converging subsequence, the functional G is strongly lower
semicontinuous in Lp/d(Ω).
Therefore, we can use Theorem 4.2.3 to have weak lower semicontinuity of G on Lp/d(Ω).
We can now apply the weak lower semicontinuity ofG to our weakly converging sequence
of minors, vk =M(Duk)⇀M(Du) =v:

§12.1 Proof on Lower Semicontinuity of the Minors
Proof of Proposition 11.2.2. We focus on the case m = 2, without loss of generality, we
can assume ℓ =d = 2 as well. Let p> 2 and suppose uk ⇀u weakly in W 1,p(Ω; R2).
Step 1: Integration by Parts (Piola Identity) for m = 2. For any smooth function

Proof of Proposition (General Case for m-th Order Minors). We proceed by induction
on the order of the minor, m.
Base Case (m = 1): This is trivially the weak convergence of the gradient Duk ⇀Du
in Lp(Ω), which holds by assumption. The case m = 2 was also fully established in the
previous step.
Induction Hypothesis: Assume that for a given integer m, the claim holds for all
(m− 1)-th order minors. Specifically, if uk ⇀u weakly in W 1,p with p>m − 1, then the
(m− 1)-th order minors converge weakly:
Mm−1(Duk)⇀Mm−1(Du) weakly in Lp/(m−1)(Ω).
Inductive Step: We now show the result holds for m-th order minors assuming p>m .
LetMαβ(Du) be the specific m×m minor corresponding to rows α = (α1,...,α m) and
columns β = (β1,...,β m). In the language of differential forms, this minor satisfies the
relation:
Mαβ(Du)dx1∧···∧ dxd =duα1∧···∧ duαm∧ ˆdxβ,
where ˆdxβ denotes the wedge product of all standard basis differentials dx1∧···∧ dxd
excluding dxβ1,...,dx βm.
Because the exterior derivative of a differential is zero ( d2 = 0), we can express the
m-form as an exact differential:
duα1∧···∧ duαm =d(uα1duα2∧···∧ duαm).
Multiplying by a smooth test function φ∈ C1
c (Ω) and integrating by parts (which is
equivalent to applying Stokes’ Theorem for forms), the derivative shifts onto φ:

Lemma 12.1.1 (Div-Curl Lemma)
Let B1⊂ Rd be a ball, and let the vector fields Uk,Vk ⇀U,V weakly in L2(B1; Rd).
Assume that:
1. div Uk = 0 in the sense of distributions,
2. curl Vk = 0 in the sense of distributions (i.e.,Vk =∇fk for some scalar functions
fk).
Then, the inner product converges in the sense of distributions:

ˆ

φ⟨V,U⟩dx ∀φ∈C∞

Remark 12.1.2 (Connection to Weak Continuity of Minors) . The weak continuity of the
2× 2 determinant, which we proved in the previous proposition, is actually the classic,
motivating example for the Div-Curl Lemma.

§12.2 Lower Semicontinuity for Quasiconvex Functions at
1 < p < +∞
Theorem 12.2.1 (Marcellini)
Let Ω⊂ Rd be an open bounded set with Lipschitz boundary. Let f : Rl⊗Rd→ R be
a continuous and quasiconvex function satisfying the polynomial growth condition:
0≤f(ξ)≤C(1 +|ξ|p)
for some 1≤p< ∞ and a constantC >0. Then, the functionalF(u) =
ˆ
Ω
f(Du)dx
is sequentially lower semicontinuous (l.s.c.) with respect to the weak W 1,p conver-
gence.
Remark 12.2.2. If p = 1, the statement is positive. One may consult Theorem 8.4 of
[Dac07] for the proof.
Theorem 12.2.3 (Acerbi-Fusco)
The same lower semicontinuity result is true also for integrands depending explicitly
on the spatial variable and the function value, i.e., f(x,z,p ).
Remark 12.2.4. The polynomial growth theorem above is not completely satisfactory
since it excludes energy functionals which are highly relevant in physical applications,
such as in nonlinear elasticity. Examples of such excluded energies are:
• Energies for Neo-Hookean materials:
ˆ
Ω
 
|Du|2 +g(detDu)

dx,
where g is a convex function.
• More general polyconvex energies:
ˆ
Ω
g(Du,M2(Du), detDu)dx,
where Du∈ R3⊗ R3.
In these physical models, the function g(A,M,t ) (where t = detDu) typically exhibits
singular behavior to prevent infinite compression or infinite expansion. As sketched in
the margins, we require the energy to blow up at the extremes:
g(A,M,t )→∞ as t→∞
and
g(A,M,t )→∞ as t→ 0+
This required singular behavior as detDu→ 0+ clearly violates the global polynomial
upper bound|f(ξ)|≤ C(1 +|ξ|p) assumed in Marcellini’s Theorem.
We split the proof into three steps with the first two steps provided in this lecture. The
third step is contained in Lecture 13.

Calculus of V ariations 2025-2026
Proof of Theorem 12.2.1.
Step 1: Establishing the local Lipschitz bound.
Since f is quasiconvex, it is necessarily rank-one convex. Rank-one convexity means
t7→f(A +t(a⊗b)) is convex for any vectors a,b . In particular, by choosing the standard
basis vectors ei and ej, f is convex with respect to each individual matrix component
(component-wise convex).
To see how the growth condition|f(ξ)|≤ C(1 +|ξ|p) yields the bound on the difference
quotient, we first isolate the mechanism in one dimension.
Lemma 12.2.5 (1D Growth to Derivative Bound)
Let h : R→ R be a convex function satisfying |h(t)|≤ M(1 +|t|p) for some p≥ 1.
Then there exists a constant c> 0 such that for all s,t∈ R:
|h(t)−h(s)|≤ c(1 +|t|p−1 +|s|p−1)|t−s|.
Proof of Lemma. By symmetry, we can assume without loss of generality that 0 ≤s<t
(the reader is invited to be assured our assumption is enough). We split this into cases
based on the location of s and t:
Case 1 (1≤s<t ): By the monotonicity of slopes for convex functions, the difference
quotient between s and t is bounded above by the slope of the secant line from t to 2t:
h(t)−h(s)
t−s ≤ h(2t)−h(t)
2t−t = h(2t)−h(t)
t .
Applying the polynomial growth bound to the numerator, and noting that 1
t≤ 1≤tp−1
since t≥ 1:
h(2t)−h(t)
t ≤ M(1 + 2ptp) +M(1 +tp)
t ≤M′tp
t =M′tp−1.
To bound the absolute value, we also need a lower bound. Looking to the left towards 0:
h(t)−h(s)
t−s ≥ h(s)−h(0)
s− 0 ≥−M(1 +sp)−M
s ≥−M′′sp−1.
Therefore,

h(t)−h(s)
t−s
≤ max(M′tp−1,M′′sp−1)≤c(tp−1 +sp−1).
Case 2 ( 0≤s<t ≤ 1): Convex functions are locally Lipschitz on bounded domains.
Since h is bounded on [−1, 2] by our growth condition, it is Lipschitz continuous on the
compact subinterval [0, 1] with some constant L. Hence:
|h(t)−h(s)|≤ L|t−s|≤ L(1 +tp−1 +sp−1)|t−s|.
Case 3 (0≤s≤ 1≤t): We simply bridge the gap by adding and subtracting h(1):
|h(t)−h(s)|≤| h(t)−h(1)| +|h(1)−h(s)|,
and apply Case 1 to the first term and Case 2 to the second. The lemma is proved. ■

Returning to Step 1: Let ξ,η ∈ Rℓ⊗ Rd. Because f is component-wise convex, we
can move from η to ξ by changing exactly one matrix component at a time. This creates
a path of N =ℓ×d steps: η =M0,M 1,...,M N =ξ.
For each step k, the matrices Mk and Mk−1 differ only in one coordinate. Let’s call the
scalar value of this coordinate t and s, respectively. The function restricted to this line,
h(τ) =f(Mk−1 + (τ−s)ei⊗ej), is a 1D convex function.
Because the other components are fixed along this line, the growth condition becomes
|h(τ)|≤ C′(1 +|τ|p). Applying our 1D Lemma to each of the N steps and summing
them up (using the triangle inequality and the equivalence of norms in finite-dimensional
spaces) immediately yields the desired multidimensional bound:
|f(ξ)−f(η)|≤ c(1 +|ξ|p−1 +|η|p−1)|ξ−η|.
■

Interior Regularity and Linearization
Global regularity on Ω depends strictly on the smoothness of both the boundary ∂Ω
and the boundary data g. Instead, we focus on interior regularity on subdomains
Ω′⋐ Ω. By a standard covering argument, it suffices to understand the regularity when
the domain is a ball BR(x).
To investigate the regularity of u, let us assume u is smooth enough to differentiate the
EL equation. Differentiating with respect to an arbitrary directional vector e∈ Sd−1
yields:
∂e
 
div(Df(Du))

= 0 =⇒ div(D2f(Du)D(∂eu)) = 0.
If we define v = ∂eu and the matrix A(x) = D2f(Du(x)), we see that the directional
derivativev solves a linear elliptic equation:
div(A(x)Dv) = 0.
Because f is uniformly elliptic, the coefficient matrix A(x) is also uniformly elliptic:
⟨A(x)ξ,ξ⟩≥ λ|ξ|2 for all ξ∈ Rd.
The Schauder Bootstrapping Mechanism
The core idea of Schauder Theory is that H¨ older continuous coefficients elevate the
regularity of the solution:
A∈Ck,α(BR(x)) =⇒ v∈Ck+1,α(BR/2(x)).
If we can somehow prove that the gradient is H¨ older continuous (i.e.,Du∈Cα, which
implies A∈Cα since f is smooth), this triggers a powerful bootstrapping loop:
A∈Cα =⇒ v∈C1,α
=⇒ u∈C2,α (since v =Du)
=⇒ A∈C1,α (since A(x) =D2f(Du(x)))
=⇒ v∈C2,α
=⇒ u∈C3,α
=⇒ A∈C2,α =⇒ ...
Through this iteration, the solution bootstraps to C∞. The critical, non-trivial missing
link is establishing that initial Cα regularity for A(x), which is exactly what the De
Giorgi-Nash-Moser theorem provides.

§14.1 Statement and Outline of Proof for the Theorem
From the previous lecture, we understood our preliminary ingredients on deducting the
regularity of a minimizer. We shall state the result of De Giorgi-Nash-Moser which will
be our main goal for this part of the lectures.
Theorem 14.1.1 (De Giorgi-Schauder)
Let f : Rd→ R such that:
1. f is smooth (analytic).
2. f is strictly convex. More specifically, we assume f is uniformly elliptic,
meaning there exist constants 0 <λ ≤ Λ<∞ such that:
λId≤D2f(ξ)≤ ΛId ∀ξ∈ Rd.
Then, for any bounded open set Ω ⊂ Rd and g∈ W 1,2(Ω), there exists a unique
minimizer u∈g +W 1,2
0 (Ω) such that
ˆ

f(Du)dx = min
v∈g+W 1,2
0 (Ω)
ˆ

f(Dv)dx
and u∈C∞(Ω) (analytic).
Remark 14.1.2. Utilizing the result of Exercise 13.2.1, we have f coercive in the sense of
Theorem 6.2.1 and thus we have a minimizer u∈g +W 1,2
0 (Ω). Moreover, from strict
convexity, the results of Proposition 6.2.4 indeed ensure the uniqueness of the minimizer
and any minimizer satisfies the property:
u is a minimizer ⇐⇒ u solves − div(Df(Du)) = 0 in the weak sense (EL).
This theorem focuses on the regularity of u, which will be proven analytic.
Remark 14.1.3. The statement of smoothness for u is false in the vectorial case (systems
of equations). Partial regularity theory is required instead.
Note that we are dealing with interior regularity. By a standard covering argument, we
can assume Ω = BR(x0). Up to the change of variable and rescaling u(x)∈W 1,2(BR(x0))
into ˜u(x) = u(Rx +x0)
R ∈ W 1,2(B1(0)), we can assume our domain is the unit ball
Ω =B1(0).
Our proof will be based on the Schauder bootstrapping mechanism. Our strategy relies
on differentiating the Euler-Lagrange equation with respect to an arbitrary directional
vectore∈ Sd−1:
∂e(div(Df(Du))) = 0 =⇒ div(D2f(Du)D(∂eu)) = 0.

If we denote v =∂eu, we see that v solves a linear elliptic equation:
− div(A(x)Dv) = 0
where A(x) =D2f(Du(x)). Because f is uniformly elliptic, the coefficient matrix A(x)
is uniformly elliptic as well (λId≤A(x)≤ ΛId). The result of Schauder, which we use
strictly for bootstrapping (stated here without proof), is as follows.
Theorem 14.1.4 (Schauder Theory)
Let A ∈ Ck,α(B1) be a coefficient matrix satisfying λId ≤ A ≤ ΛId for some
0<λ< Λ. Then, every weak solution of− div(A(x)Dv) = 0 belongs toCk+1,α(B1/2)
and satisfies the estimate:
∥v∥Ck+1,α(B1/2)≤C(λ, Λ, [A]Ck,α(B1))·∥v∥W 1,2(B1).
Remark 14.1.5. Schauder Theory is fundamentally a perturbative theory. By freezing the
coefficients at a point x0, one can rewrite the operator as −∆v = div((A(x)−Id)Dv).
If A(x) is continuous, the perturbation term (A(x)−Id) is arbitrarily small in a small
neighborhood, allowing the smoothness of the Laplace operator to transfer to v. However,
ifA(x) is only bounded and measurable, this error isO(1), and the perturbation argument
fails.
Following Schauder’s result, the bootstrapping mechanism activates formally:
Corollary 14.1.6
Let f and u be as in Theorem 14.1.1. If u∈C1,α(B1), then u∈C∞(B1/2).
Indeed, Theorem 14.1.1 is rigorously proved if we can establish the missing link: that
v =∂eu∈C0,α for any direction e. We will reach this final goal by proving the following
monumental theorem.
Theorem 14.1.7 (De Giorgi-Nash-Moser Theorem)
Let A(x) be a measurable matrix satisfying λId≤A(x)≤ ΛId. If v∈W 1,2(B1) is a
weak solution of− div(A(x)Dv) = 0, then there exist constants β =β(d,λ, Λ)> 0
and C =C(d,λ, Λ)> 0 such that v∈C0,β(B1/2) and

2. For dimension d = 2, establishing H¨ older continuity directly via the hole-filling

Remark 14.1.8 (The One-Dimensional Case). In dimensiond = 1, the advanced machinery

ordinary differential equation:

and therefore globally invertible. Applying the inverse yields a constant gradient:
u′(x) = (f′)−1(c) =m
Integrating this shows that any minimizer must be an affine function, u(x) =mx +k.
Since affine functions are trivially C∞ (and analytic), the interior regularity problem is
immediately solved without requiring energy bounds or bootstrapping.

15 Hilbert’s 19th Problem - 2
§15.1 Higher Regularity for Solutions to − div(Df(Du)) = 0
In the previous lecture, we established the existence of a minimizer u∈W 1,2(Ω) for the
functional
ˆ
Ω
f(Du)dx and observed that if the gradient Du is locally H¨ older continuous,
Schauder’s perturbative theory automatically bootstraps the regularity to C∞.
To bridge the gap between u∈W 1,2 and Du∈C0,α
loc , we decompose our main objective
(Theorem 14.1.7) into two distinct, highly non-trivial regularity results.
Theorem 15.1.1 (W 2,2 Regularity)
Let f be an uniformly elliptic Lagrangian such that λId ≤ D2f(ξ)≤ ΛId. If
u∈W 1,2(B1) is a weak solution to the non-linear Euler-Lagrange equation
− div(Df(Du)) = 0,
then u∈W 2,2
loc (B1). Consequently, the gradient Du admits weak derivatives in L2
loc.
Theorem 15.1.2 (De Giorgi for Linear Equations)
LetA(x) be a bounded, measurable coefficient matrix satisfying the uniform ellipticity
conditionλId≤A(x)≤ ΛId. If v∈W 1,2(B1) is a weak solution to the linear equation
− div(A(x)Dv) = 0,
then v∈C0,α
loc (B1) for some exponent α∈ (0, 1) depending only on d,λ, and Λ.
The full regularity of the minimizer follows immediately from the concatenation of these
two theorems.
Assume u∈W 1,2(B1) is our minimizer. By Theorem 15.1.1, we gain the exact regularity
needed to differentiate the Euler-Lagrange equation. Specifically, since u∈W 2,2
loc (B1),
the directional derivative v =∂eu is well-defined as a function in W 1,2
loc (B1).
Differentiating the weak formulation− div(Df(Du)) = 0 with respect to an arbitrary
direction e∈ Sd−1 yields:
− div(D2f(Du)D(∂eu)) = 0 =⇒ −div(A(x)Dv) = 0,
where A(x) = D2f(Du(x)). Because f is uniformly elliptic, A(x) strictly satisfies
λId≤A(x)≤ ΛId.
At this stage, v =∂eu∈W 1,2
loc solves a linear uniformly elliptic equation with measurable
coefficients. We may therefore apply Theorem 15.1.2, which immediately grants v∈C0,α
loc .
Since this holds for any direction e, the full gradient is H¨ older continuous:Du∈C0,α
loc .
89

===== PAGE 96 =====
Orlando F errari
refrainfr.github.io
Lecture Notes on
Calculus of V ariations 2025-2026
Once Du∈C0,α
loc , the coefficient matrix A(x) =D2f(Du(x)) is also C0,α
loc . This activates
the Schauder bootstrapping mechanism introduced in the previous lecture, elevating u to
C2,α, then C3,α, and ultimately C∞.
The remainder of this section is strictly devoted to proving Theorem 15.1.1.
To rigorously prove Theorem 15.1.1, we must show that the second weak derivatives of u
exist. However, we cannot directly differentiate the Euler-Lagrange equation because
we do not yet know if D2u exists. To bypass this circular dependency, we must utilize
Nirenberg’s method of finite difference quotients.
For a function u(x), a unit vector e∈ Sd−1, and a non-zero scalar h∈ R, we define the
finite difference quotient as:
δe,hu(x) = u(x +he)−u(x)
h .
We take h to be sufficiently small in absolute value so that the shifted points remain
inside the domain. The following foundational lemma from functional analysis establishes
that bounding these difference quotients is perfectly equivalent to possessing a weak
derivative.
Lemma 15.1.3 (Translation Lemma / Difference Quotients)
Let p∈ (1, +∞] and let B1 be the unit ball centered at the origin. For any function
u∈Lp(B1), the following statements are equivalent:
1. u∈W 1,p(B1/2).
2. There exists a constant C >0 such that for any direction e∈ Sd−1 and any
scalar 0 <|h|< 1/2, the difference quotient satisfies ∥δe,hu∥Lp(B1/2)≤C.
Furthermore, if statement (1) holds, the constant in (2) can be taken as C =
∥Du∥Lp(B1).

weak compactness of bounded sets in Lp. For the boundary case p = 1, the space L1 lacks
this weak compactness property. In that scenario, condition (2) instead characterizes the
space of functions of Bounded Variation, u∈BV (B1/2).

§15.2 Caccioppoli Inequality on Level Sets
To prove Theorem 15.1.2 (H¨ older continuity for linear equations), we cannot rely on
the standard Caccioppoli inequality. As we saw in the previous lecture, the hole-filling
technique derived from the global Caccioppoli inequality fails to yield H¨ older continuity
in dimensions d≥ 3.
To bypass this dimensional barrier, De Giorgi introduced a profound shift in perspective:
instead of bounding the energy of the entire function v, we must bound the energy of its
super-level sets (the parts of the function that exceed a certain height k).
Definition 15.2.1 (Truncation)
For any constantk∈ R, we define the positive truncated function as:
(v−k)+ = max(v−k, 0) = (v−k)∨ 0.
By Stampacchia’s Lemma (chain rule for Sobolev space), if v∈ W 1,2(Ω), then
(v−k)+∈W 1,2(Ω) and its weak gradient is given by:
D(v−k)+ =
(
Dv almost everywhere on{x∈ Ω :v(x)>k}
0 almost everywhere on {x∈ Ω :v(x)≤k}
This allows us to establish a localized Caccioppoli inequality specifically for subsolutions.
Theorem 15.2.2 (Level-Set Caccioppoli Inequality)
Let A(x) be a measurable matrix satisfying the uniform ellipticity condition λId≤
A(x)≤ ΛId. Let v ∈ W 1,2(B1) be a weak subsolution to the linear equation,
meaning:

§16.1 Symmetries and the L2− L∞ Bound
Before we proceed to the De Giorgi iteration, we establish several fundamental stability
properties of solutions and subsolutions under elementary transformations. These sym-
metries allow us to assume, without loss of generality, that our domains are unit balls
and our solutions are non-negative and properly scaled.
Recall that v∈W 1,2 is a subsolution to our linear equation if − div(A(x)Dv)≤ 0 in the
weak sense, meaning:
ˆ
⟨A(x)Dv,Dφ⟩ dx≤ 0 ∀φ∈W 1,2
0 ,φ≥ 0.
Exercise 16.1.1
Assume A(x) is uniformly elliptic ( λ Id ≤ A(x) ≤ Λ Id). Prove the following
symmetries:
1. Translation, Scaling, and Multiplication: If v is a subsolution (resp.
supersolution) in BR(x0), then for any α >0, the rescaled function ˜v(y) =
αv(x0 +Ry) is a subsolution (resp. supersolution) in B1(0) with respect to
the rescaled matrix ˜A(y) =A(x0 +Ry).
2. Sign Reversal: v is a subsolution if and only if −v is a supersolution.
3. Composition (Subsolutions): Ifv is a subsolution, thenf(v) is a subsolution
for any functionf∈C2(R) that is monotonically increasing (f′≥ 0) and convex
(f′′≥ 0).
4. Composition (Solutions): Ifv is a exact solution, then f(v) is a subsolution
for any convex function f∈C2(R) (monotonicity is no longer required).
Solution.
1. Let φ∈ W 1,2
0 (B1(0)) with φ≥ 0. We change variables x = x0 +Ry. Note that
Dy˜v(y) = αRDv(x), Dyφ(y) = RDxφ(x), and dy = R−ddx. Substituting these
into the weak formulation for ˜v on B1(0) yields:
ˆ

˜A(y)Dy˜v(y)·Dyφ(y)dy =αR2−d
ˆ
BR(x0)
A(x)Dv(x)·Dxφ(x)dx≤ 0,
confirming ˜v is a subsolution.
2. This follows trivially from the linearity of the integral and the gradient: D(−v) =
−Dv. Multiplying the weak inequality by −1 flips the inequality sign, exchanging
subsolutions for supersolutions.
3. To showf(v) is a subsolution, we must show
ˆ
AD(f(v))·Dφ≤ 0 for any φ≥ 0.
Using the chain rule, D(f(v)) =f′(v)Dv.

§16.1.1 The Maximum Principle and the Necessity of the Local Bound
To understand why the De Giorgi iteration is necessary, consider a variational perspective.
The weak solution v is a minimizer of the energy functional
ˆ
⟨A(x)Dv,Dv⟩ dx≃
ˆ
|Dv|2dx
over the space g +W 1,2
0 (B1), where g is fixed boundary data.
Suppose the boundary data is globally bounded by a constant: sup
∂B1
g≤ K. Consider
the truncated function w = v∧K. Since w matches v on the boundary, it is a valid
competitor. Because the gradient of w vanishes wherever v > K, its total energy is
strictly less than v unless v≤K everywhere. Since v is the minimizer, we immediately
conclude that sup
B1
v≤K.
However, this classical Maximum Principle requires explicit knowledge that the boundary
data is bounded (L∞). If we only know that v∈W 1,2
loc , we have no boundary control. De
Giorgi’s monumental realization was that the Level-Set Caccioppoli inequality is sufficient
to force the function to be locally bounded strictly in the interior, relying only on its L2
mass.

Remark 16.1.5 (Historical Literature) . The techniques developed here trace back to the
foundational works in regularity theory. For the original proof, see De Giorgi’s seminal
1957 paper [ DG57]. For modern, highly streamlined treatments applying these methods
to fluid dynamics and general PDEs, excellent resources include the notes by Caffarelli
and Vasseur [CV10], and the comprehensive text by Ambrosio, Carlotto, and Massaccessi
[ACM18].
§16.2 Oscillation Lemma - 1
To finalize the proof of De Giorgi’s Theorem (Theorem 15.1.2), it is sufficient to prove
that the oscillation of the solution strictly drops when passing to a smaller inner ball.
We state this as the following central claim.

From Discrete Decay to Continuous Oscillation
Iterating this inequality k times over shrinking radii Rk = 4−k centered at an arbitrary
point x0 yields:
oscB4−k(x0)v≤

We can express this discrete decay factor as a spatial power law. Define the H¨ older
exponent α =− log4(1−η/2)> 0. By definition, this means (1 −η/2)k = (4−k)α.
Now, let r be any arbitrary continuous radius such that 0 < r≤ 1/4. We can trap r
between two successive dyadic radii by finding an integer k≥ 1 such that 4−(k+1)<r ≤
4−k. Because the oscillation of a function monotonically decreases as the domain shrinks,
we have:
oscBr(x0)v≤ oscB4−k(x0)v≤ (4−k)α oscB1(x0)v.
Using the lower bound 4−k = 4·4−(k+1)< 4r, we obtain the continuous oscillation bound:
oscBr(x0)v≤ (4r)α oscB1(x0)v =Crα.

Remark 16.2.2 (From Oscillation to Pointwise H¨ older Continuity). To see why this local
oscillation bound forces the standard pointwise H¨ older condition everywhere in B1/2,
consider any two points x,y∈B1/2.
• Close points (|x−y|≤ 1/4): Let r =|x−y|. Because x∈B1/2, its distance to
∂B1 is at least 1/2, so the “sliding microscope” ball B1/4(x) safely fits inside the
domain. Both x and y lie in Br(x), meaning their difference is bounded by the
oscillation on that ball:
|v(x)−v(y)|≤ oscBr(x)v≤Crα =C|x−y|α.
• Far points (|x−y|> 1/4): The L2−L∞ estimate guarantees v is bounded. Let
M = sup
B1/2
|v|. The maximum possible difference is 2 M. Since 4|x−y| > 1, we
trivially have 1 < (4|x−y|)α, meaning:
|v(x)−v(y)|≤ 2M <2M4α|x−y|α.
Taking the maximum of the constants from both cases rigorously proves v∈Cα(B1/2),
officially completing the proof of Theorem 15.1.2.
Furthermore, we can explicitly bound the final H¨ older constant by the initial energy
of the system. Recall from our continuous decay formula that the H¨ older constant C
depends directly on the initial oscillation and the supremum of v. By applying the
L2−L∞ estimate (Proposition 16.1.2) and the Oscillation Bound Corollary 16.1.3, we
can control both sup v and oscv entirely by the L2 average of v.
Restoring the arbitrary radius R via standard scaling symmetry (Exercise 16.1.1), we
obtain the complete, scale-invariant form of the De Giorgi regularity estimate:
∥v∥L∞(BR/2(x0)) +Rα[v]C0,α(BR/2(x0))≤C(d,λ, Λ)

This successfully recovers the exact quantitative bound stated in the De Giorgi-Nash-
Moser theorem (Theorem 14.1.7).
§16.2.2 Bridging the Gap: The Intermediate Measure Lemma
To prove Claim 16.2.1, we recall the specific consequence of the L2−L∞ bound (Fast
Convergence Lemma) established in the previous section.
Remark 16.2.3 (The “Tiny Measure” Condition) . From Proposition 16.1.2 and its recur-
rence relation, we know there exists a strictly positive constant δ =δ(d, Λ/λ) such that
if v is a subsolution, v≤ 1 in B1, and the measure of its positive part is sufficiently tiny:
|{v >0}∩ B1/2|≤ δ,
then the supremum strictly drops in the inner ball: sup
B1/4
v≤ 1/2.
Derivation of δ: We apply the scale-invariant L2−L∞ estimate (Proposition 16.1.2)
starting from the ball B1/2 instead of B1. Because v≤ 1, its energy on this domain is
trivially bounded by its active support area:
∥v+∥2

Lemma 16.2.4 (Measure-to-Pointwise Lemma)
For everyε> 0, there exists η =η(ε,d, Λ/λ)∈ (0, 1) such that if v∈W 1,2(B1) is a
subsolution to− div(A(x)Dv)≤ 0 satisfying:

|{v≥ 1−η}∩ B1/2|≤ ε.
The proof of this lemma requires the Measure/Poincar´ e inequality (which we will cover
in the next lecture). For now, we will assume Lemma 16.2.4 holds and use it to formally
construct the proof of the main claim.
Proof of Claim 16.2.1. Let δ =δ(d, Λ/λ) be the universal constant from Remark 16.2.3.

§17.1 Oscillation Lemma - 2: The Measure/Poincar´ e Inequality

that lemma, we must first establish a variation of the Poincar´ e inequality that controls
the L1 norm of a function based on the size of its zero-set.

§17.2 Closing the Proof: The Measure-to-Pointwise Lemma
We now possess all the required machinery to rigorously prove the aforementioned Lemma
16.2.4, officially resolving Hilbert’s 19th Problem.
Proof of Lemma 16.2.4. We define an infinite sequence of height levels kn→ 1:
kn = 1− 2−n for n = 0, 1, 2,...
Notice that k0 = 0, kn+1−kn = 2−(n+1), and 1−kn = 2−n.
We apply Corollary 17.1.4 to the function v on the ball B1/2, using the height levels
ℓ =kn and h =kn+1. By assumption, |{v≤ 0}|≥ 1

Remark 17.2.1 (The End of the Bootstrapping Loop) . With Lemma 16.2.4 proven, Claim
16.2.1 is completely rigorous. The oscillation of the directional derivative v = ∂eu
geometrically decays, proving v∈ Cα. Schauder theory activates, bootstrapping the
minimizer u∈W 1,2 to u∈C∞.
Hilbert’s 19th Problem is officially solved.

§17.3 The Vectorial Case: De Giorgi’s Counterexample
Throughout our proof of Hilbert’s 19th Problem, we assumed that our minimizer u is
a scalar-valued function (u: Rd→ R). A natural question arises: does this magnificent
regularity theory hold for systems of equations, where u is vector-valued (u: Rd→ Rℓ for
ℓ> 1)?
In 1968, De Giorgi proved that the answer is definitively no for dimensions ℓ≥ 3. He
constructed a brilliant counterexample demonstrating that weak solutions to linear elliptic
systems with bounded measurable coefficients can be unbounded, meaning the crucial
L2−L∞ estimate fails completely in the vectorial case.
Theorem 17.3.1 (De Giorgi’s Counterexample (1968))
Let ℓ≥ 3. There exists a tensor of coefficients Aij
αβ(x) (for spatial indices i,j =
1,...,d and vectorial indices α,β = 1,...,m ) that is symmetric and uniformly
elliptic:
λ Id≤Aij
αβ(x)≤ Λ Id
and a vector-valued function u∈W 1,2(B1; Rℓ) that weakly solves the linear elliptic
system:
div(A(x)Du) = 0 in B1
such that u /∈L∞
loc(B1; Rℓ).
In particular, u is a minimizer of the strictly convex quadratic functional
ˆ
B1
⟨A(x)Du,Du⟩ dx,
yet it fails to be continuous.
Remark 17.3.2 (Given by Gemini) . In his original paper, De Giorgi takes ℓ = d and
explicitly builds the unbounded solution in the form:
u(x) = x
|x|γ
for a carefully chosen exponent γ >0. Because the origin acts as a singularity where the
function blows up to infinity, it is impossible to establish the Oscillation Lemma. For
vectorial problems, one must abandon the hope of everywhere-regularity and instead
rely on partial regularity theory, which guarantees that solutions are smooth everywhere
except on a singular set of measure zero.

§18.2 Introduction to Γ-Convergence
The rigorous framework to formalize these asymptotic behaviors is Γ-convergence, intro-
duced by De Giorgi. Let ( X,d ) be a metric space (or more generally, (X,τ ) a topological
space). Let Fε :X→ R∪{ +∞}. We define the sequence of minimization problems and
the limit problem as:
(Pε) : inf {Fε(u) :u∈X}
(P0) : inf {F0(u) :u∈X}
Our key requirements to say that Fε
Γ− →F0 are:
1. If uε is an almost minimizer of ( Pε), meaning Fε(uε) = inf
v
Fε(v) +oε(1), and
uε→ ¯u, we want ¯u to be a minimizer of (P0).
2. If G is a d-continuous functional, we want the almost minimizers of Fε +G to
converge to the minimizers of F0 +G.
Moreover, we also consider other ingredients where we want the infimal values to converge
properly:
1. ∀{vε}⊂ X : F0(¯u) = inf{F0(v)} = lim
ε→0
inf
u
{Fε(u)} = lim
ε→0
Fε(uε)≤ lim inf
ε→0
Fε(vε),
where uε is a minimizer sequence.
2. If uε are a.e. minimizers, their energies should converge: Fε(uε)→F0(¯u).
Remark 18.2.1. Note that for any function F and any target point ¯u, a standard
penalization approach ensures:
uλ∈ argmin{F (u) +λd(u, ¯u)} λ→∞−−−→¯u
To formalize this, let us properly package both requirements into the strict definition of
Γ-convergence.
Definition 18.2.2 (Γ-Convergence)
Let (X,d ) be a metric space (or more generally, a topological space ( X,τ )). Let
Fε :X→ R and F :X→ R. We say Fε
Γ− →F if both of the following conditions
hold:
1. (Γ-liminf inequality): For every εk↘ 0, for every u∈ X, and for every
sequence uεk→u, we have:
F (u)≤ lim inf
k→∞
Fεk(uεk)
2. (Γ-limsup inequality): For everyu∈X, there exists a sequence uεk→u
such that:
F (u)≥ lim sup
k→∞
Fεk(uεk)
Remark 18.2.3. Several immediate structural consequences follow from this definition:
1. By coupling condition (1) with condition (2), the limit supremum in (2) is actually
a strict limit.

Theorem 18.2.4 (Fundamental Theorem of Γ-Convergence)
Assume Fε

a) Fε(uε)− inf

Proof. Pick any arbitrary v∈X and let vεk be a recovery sequence for v (guaranteed by
the Γ-limsup inequality). We can evaluate the energy bounds as k→∞ :
O(1) +F (v)

Note that the middle inequality holds because uεk is almost minimizing. Taking the limit
as k→∞ , we simply obtain F (¯u)≤F (v). Since v∈X was arbitrary, ¯u is a minimizer
of F . ■

§19.2 Epigraphical Convergence (Kuratowski)
Definition 19.2.1 (Kuratowski Convergence of Sets)
Let (Y,d ) be a metric space and {Ck}⊆P (Y ). We say that Ck→C∈P (Y ) in the
Kuratowski sense (denoted Ck
K−→C) if:
1. ∀xk∈Ck, if xk→x, then x∈C.
2. ∀x∈C,∃xk∈Ck such that xk→x.
Exercise 19.2.2
Prove that if Ck
K−→C, then C is automatically a closed set.
118

===== PAGE 125 =====
Orlando F errari
refrainfr.github.io
Lecture Notes on
Calculus of V ariations 2025-2026
Proof. Let ¯x∈ ¯C. There exists a sequence x(j)∈C such that x(j)→ ¯x as j→∞ . By
the second property of Kuratowski convergence, for each fixed j, there exists a sequence
x(j)
k ∈Ck such that x(j)
k →x(j) as k→∞ . By a standard diagonalization procedure, we
can extract a sequence k(j)→∞ such that yj :=x(j)
k(j)→ ¯x. Since yj∈Ck(j), the first
property of Kuratowski convergence implies ¯x∈C. Thus C is closed. ■
Proposition 19.2.3
Fk
Γ− →F ⇐⇒ epiFk
K−→epiF , where epiFk ={(x,t )∈X× R :Fk(x)≤t}.
Proof. Assume Fk
Γ− →F .
• Let (xk,tk)∈ epiFk converge to (x,t ). Then Fk(xk)≤tk. Taking the liminf and
applying the Γ-liminf inequality gives F (x)≤ lim infFk(xk)≤ lim inftk =t. Hence
(x,t )∈ epiF .
• Let (x,t )∈ epiF , so F (x)≤t. By the Γ-limsup inequality, there exists a recovery
sequence xk → x with lim supFk(xk) ≤ F (x) ≤ t. By selecting a sequence
tk≥Fk(xk) such that tk→t, we construct (xk,tk)∈ epiFk converging to (x,t ).
The reverse implication follows symmetrically by translating Kuratowski sequences into
Γ-recovery sequences and liminf bounds. ■
Remark 19.2.4.
1. Γ-convergence is the convergence of epigraphs, not of graphs!
2. Ck
K−→C ⇐⇒ Ck
K−→C.
3. epi ¯F = epiF , which is exactly the l.s.c. envelope.

§21.1 Introduction and the Unscaled Functional
Consider a material occupying a domain Ω ⊂ Rd that can exist in two distinct phases.
We describe the state of the material by a phase parameter u : Ω→ [−1, 1], where the
pure phases correspond to the sets {u =−1} and{u = 1}.
Typically, the phase separation is driven by a double-well potential. A standard choice is:
W (t) = (1−t2)2

potential behaves quadratically: W (t)∼C(t∓ 1)2 +O((t∓ 1)3).

§21.2.1 The Leap of Faith and Sets of Finite Perimeter
Minimizing over all possible profiles f, the sharp lower bound is dictated by the optimal

Remark 21.2.2. In the heuristic above, we performed a “leap of faith” by assuming that
the interface Σ is smooth enough to admit a tubular neighborhood and well-defined
normal distances. In reality, sequences of bounded energy may converge to characteristic
functions 1E− 1Ec where the set E is highly irregular.
To formalize this in Calculus of Variations, we require the geometric measure theory of
Sets of Finite Perimeter (Caccioppoli sets). The perimeter of a measurable set E in
Ω is defined via distributional derivatives as:
Per(E, Ω) = sup
ˆ
E
divφdx :φ∈C1
c (Ω; Rd),∥φ∥∞≤ 1

.
131

===== PAGE 138 =====
Orlando F errari
refrainfr.github.io
Lecture Notes on
Calculus of V ariations 2025-2026
This definition does not require ∂E to be a manifold. If E has a smooth boundary,
Per(E, Ω) =Hd−1(∂E∩Ω). By De Giorgi’s structure theorem, sets of finite perimeter have
a well-defined “reduced boundary” ∂∗E, over which the Coarea formula and integration
by parts rigorously hold.
This framework leads directly to the seminal result for phase transitions:
Theorem 21.2.3 (Modica-Mortola)

By construction, outside the bad intervals of measure O(2−N), the deviation|uεk−UN,k|
is large only if uεk strays from the pure phases, which we know vanishes in measure. For
fixed N, there exists K(N) such that for all k≥K(N), the total deviation measure is
bounded by 1/N.
Defining the diagonal sequence VN =UN,K(N), we observe that the sets {VN = 1} and
{VN =−1} stabilize, and|{VN = 0}| =O(2−N)→ 0. Thus, VN→u in measure. By a
standard diagonalization argument, we extract a subsequence such that uεk→u almost
everywhere. Because the number of transitions (bad intervals) is uniformly bounded,
u∈P.C.((a,b );{−1, 1}).
Step 4: Liminf Bound on Jumps.
Given the piecewise constant limit u, let{x1,...,x n} be its minimal partition of jump
points. We can choose a grid sufficiently fine such that each jump is isolated within
distinct intervals. Applying the exact same 1D local energy lower bound across each
jump isolating interval confirms that each jump contributes at least CW to the total
energy. Thus:
CW· #J(u)≤ lim inf
k→∞
Fεk(uεk).

§22.2 Γ-limsup Inequality and Optimal Profile Construction
To prove the Γ-limsup, we must construct a recovery sequenceuε
L2
−→u that asymptotically
matches the energy bound. We construct this sequence by carefully “gluing” rescaled
optimal profiles into the jump discontinuities of the target piecewise constant function u.
Construction of the Recovery Sequence. Let u ∈ P.C.((a,b );{−1, 1}). For simplicity,
assume u has a single jump at x =c, transitioning from−1 to 1.
By definition of the optimal profile cost,CW = inf

1. ¯u(0) = 0,
2. ¯u(T )≥ 1−δ and ¯u(−T )≤− 1 +δ,
3. The total energy approximates the optimal cost:
